INTRODUCTION


THE  AUTHOR’S  PREFACE.

The  Masnavi  is  thus  described  by  its  author  in  his
Arabic  preface : —

“ This  is  the  book  of  the  Masnavi.  It  contains
the  roots  of  the  roots  of  the  roots  of  the  Paith,  and
treats  of  the  mysteries  of  ‘Union’  and  ‘Certitude.’
Thus  saith  the  feeble  slave,  in  need  of  the  mercy  of
God,  whose  name  be  praised,  Muhammad,  son  of
Muhammad,  son  of  Husain,  of  Balkh,  of  whom  may
God  accept  it, — ‘ I have  exerted  myself  to  enlarge  this
book  of  poetry  in  rhymed  couplets,  which  contains
strange  and  rare  narratives,  beautiful  sayings  and
recondite  indications ; a path  for  the  devout,  and  a
garden  for  the  pious ; short  in  its  expressions,  but  having
numerous  applications.’  ” The  author  goes  on  to  state
that  he  wrote  his  book  at  the  instance  of  Shaikh  Hasan,
son  of  Muhammad,  “ a chief  of  the  Gnostics  (Arif  in)
and  a leader  of  right  direction  and  of  sure  knowledge ; ”
and  concludes  with  a prayer  that  it  may  prove  “ a
blessing  to  those  who  are  possessed  of  insight,  the
godly,  the  spiritual,  the  heavenly-minded,  the  men  of
light  who  keep  silence  and  observe,  who  are  absent  in
spirit  though  present  in  the  body,  very  kings  though
clothed  in  rags,  true  nobles  among  the  people,  endued
with  virtues,  the  lights  of  guidance.”

xiii


XIV


INTRODUCTION.


II.

SUFISM.

The  Masnavi  is  a summary  of  the  religious  senti-
ments and  doctrine  of  Islam  as  interpreted  and  modi-
fied by  the  Muhammadan  Mystics  or  Sufis.  And,  as  the
preface  declares,  it  is  specially  addressed  to  Sufis,  men
who  aimed  at  following  the  more  perfect  way  as  opposed
to  the.  moderate  practice  of  average  Muhammadans,  men
who  professed  to  renounce  the  world  and  seek  know-
ledge of  God  and  union  with  Him  in  a life  of  self-
mortification and  spiritual  meditation.  Although  some
Sufi  tenets  seem  repugnant  to  the  teaching  of  the
Koran,  nevertheless  there  is  ample  evidence  to  prove
that  the  Sufis  were  the  lineal  spiritual  descendants  of  the
earliest  saints  of  Islam,  and  were,  in  fact,  what  Catholics
call  the  “ Religious  ” of  the  Muhammadan  communion.
Haji  Ivhalfa  in  his  article  on  Sufism  ( Tasawwvf ),  quotes
Qushairi  to  the  effect  that,  after  the  death  of  Muhammad,
the  most  earnest  adherents  of  the  Faith  were  called
“ Companions  of  the  Prophet  ” (Ashdb),  their  successors
bore  the  title  of  “Followers”  ( Tabd’iyun ),  and  in  the
third  generation  this  title  was  altered  to  that  of
“ Ascetics  ” ( Zdliici ),  or  “ Devotees  ” (’ Abid ).  The  first

to  bear  the  name  “ Sufi  ” (“  clad  in  wool  ”),  was  one
Abu  Hashim,  a Syrian  ascetic,  who  died  in  150  a.h.
(767  A.D.).  Jami,  in  his  life  of  Abu  Hashim,  says  that
the  first  convent  ( Khanqdh ) was  built  for  the  Sufis  by
a Christian  nobleman  who  had  remarked  their  love  for
one  another.  This  adoption  of  quasi-monastic  institu-
tions seems  to  have  been  the  first  step  taken  by  the
Sufis  beyond  the  pale  of  strict  Muhammadan  ordinances.
The  prophet  had  said,  “There  is  no  monkery  in  Islam,”
but  this  dictum  had  to  be  ignored  or  explained  away
by  the  growing  sect.  Their  increasing  spiritual  exalta-
tion soon  led  them  to  adopt  other  modifications  of


INTRODUCTION.


xv


ordinary  Muhammadan  doctrines.  Thus  the  ordinary
doctrine  of  resignation  to  the  will  of  Allah  ( Tasllm ),
was  soon  developed  into  an  exaggerated  “ Quietism  ”
like  that  of  Mme.  Guyon  in  France.  In  his  Tazkiratu-l-
Auliyd,  ’Attar  gives  the  life  of  Rabi’a  of  Basra,  a female
saint  of  the  second  century  of  the  Hijira.  A conversa-
tion took  place  between  her  and  three  holy  men  on  the
subject  of  sincerity  (Sidy).  One  of  them  said,  “ He  is
not  sincere  who  does  not  patiently  endure  the  blows  of
his  Lord.”  Eabi’a  answered,  “ That  saying  smells  of
egotism.”  Another  said,  “ He  is  not  sincere  in  his  pro-
fessions who  does  not  enjoy  the  blow's  of  his  Lord.”
Rabi’a  rejoined,  “ I know  a better  saying  than  that :
He  is  not  sincere  who  does  not  forget  the  pain  of  the
blows  in  his  joy  at  beholding  the  face  of  his  Lord.”
Once  she  fell  sick,  and  they  asked  her  the  cause  of
her  sickness.  She  said,  “ I dwelt  upon  the  joys  of
Paradise,  and  therefore  my  Beloved  has  chastened  me.”
She  added,  “ Consume  with  fire,  0 Lord,  the  heart  that
loveth  thee.”  This  is  exactly  Mme.  Guyon’s  senti-
ment : —

“ Love,  if  thy  destined  sacrifice  am  I,

Come,  slay  thy  victim  and  prepare  thy  fires,

Plunged  in  thy  depths  of  mercy  let  me  die
The  death  which  every  soul  that  lives  desires.”

Rabi’a  was  asked  if  she  wished  to  marry ; she  said,
“ The  bonds  of  wedlock  have  descended  upon  me,  I
am  not  my  own  but  my  Lord's,  and  must  not  be  un-
faithful to  Him.”  Hasan  of  Basra  said  to  her,  “ How
dost  thou  know  Him  ? ” She  answered,  “ Thou  knowest
Him  as  such  and  such,  I as  Nonsuch.”  When  she
made  the  pilgrimage  and  saw  the  Ka’la,  she  said,  “ I
see  only  bricks  and  a house  of  stone,  what  do  they
profit  me  ? ’Tis  Thou  that  I want.”  This  saying  is,
perhaps,  the  earliest  expression  of  that  indifference  to
outward  forms  and  rites  which  distinguished  Sufis  as  a
class.


b


XVI


INTRODUCTION.


Another  famous  saint  of  the  second  century  a.h.  was
Ibrahim  bin  Adham  of  Balkh.  He  is  a typical  example
of  the  Sufi  ascetic.  Born  in  the  purple,  he  renounced
all  the  pomps  and  vanities  of  the  world  and  became  a
Darvesh.  One  story  told  of  his  ascetic  practices  is  that
he  was  not  content  to  make  the  pilgrimage  in  the
ordinary  way,  but  stopped  at  the  end  of  every  mile  of
the  road  to  prostrate  himself  a thousand  times.  By  this
and  similar  works  of  supererogation  he  of  course  acquired
supernatural  powers.  Just  as  St.  Anthony  of  Padua
constrained  the  fishes  at  Ravenna  to  listen  to  his  preach-
ing, Ibrahim  made  the  fishes  in  the  Tigris  bring  back
a needle  which  he  had  dropped  into  that  river.  The
miracles  ascribed  to  all  the  chief  Sufi  saints,  are  almost
exact  counterparts  of  those  of  St.  Francis  of  Assisi,  and
other  noted  Christian  saints.  As  Rumi  says,  “ Love
and  faith  are  a mighty  spell.”  The  “ Golden  Legend  ”
is  the  natural  outcome  of  the  love  and  reverence  in-
spired by  the  memories  of  saintly  lives.

By  the  end  of  the  second  century  of  the  Hijira,  it  is
clear  that  Sufi  doctrine  had  come  to  differ  in  some
respects  from  ordinary  Muhammadan  views,  but  there
is  good  evidence  to  prove  that  the  Sufis  of  that  time
were  highly  esteemed.  According  to  the  Tazkiratu-l-
Auliya , the  great  Canonist,  Imam  Ash-Shafi’i  once  visited
a Sufi  convent  and  listened  to  the  discourse  of  the
brethren,  and  then  gave  it  as  his  opinion  that  “ The
knowledge  (of  God)  possessed  by  the  whole  world  did
not  equal  his  own  knowledge,  but  that  his  own  know-
ledge fell  short  of  the  knowledge  possessed  by  the  Sufis.”
The  Imam  Ahmad  Hanbal,  another  of  the  great  Canonists,
gave  similar  testimony.  One  day  his  disciples  said  to
him,  “ These  Sufis  rest  on  Mind  faith  or  trust  ( tawakkal ),
but  are  devoid  of  knowledge.”  Hanbal  answered,  “ Ye
are  mistaken,  knowledge  is  implanted  in  them.”  They
said,  “ They  aspire  to  nothing  more  than  a broken  crust.”
He  replied,  “ I know  no  sect  on  the  face  of  the  earth


INTRODUCTION.


XVII


whose  aspirations  are  nobler  than  theirs,  since  they
desire  nothing  of  the  world  beyond  a broken  crust.”

In  the  third  century  of  the  Hijira,  Sufi  doctrines
were  developed,  which  at  last  brought  some  of  the  more
advanced  members  of  the  sect  into  conflict  with  the
ecclesiastical  authorities.  The  earliest  and  most  cele-
brated of  these  more  advanced  doctrinaires  was  Bayazld
of  Bistarn  in  Ivhorasan,  who  was  descended  from  a Parsi
(or  Magian)  family.  He  used  to  say  that  he  had  gained
his  spiritual  knowledge  by  au  empty  belly  and  a naked
body.  Ibn  Khalliqan  records  one  of  his  sayings  to  the
effect  that  it  is  not  the  performance  of  miracles  which
proves  saintship,  but  rather  a godly  and  righteous  life.
On  the  other  hand,  many  of  the  sayings  ascribed  to  him
in  the  “ Lives  of  the  Saints  ” are  of  the  most  extravagant
character.  He  would  say,  “ Glory  be  to  me  ! ” ( Subhdni ),
implying  that  he  was  one  with  the  Deity.  In  the
Masnavi,  Puuni  gives  some  specimens  of  these  extravagant
sayings,  and  excuses  him  on  the  ground  that  he  was
possessed  by  a spirit,  and  therefore  not  answerable.

It  seems  worth  while  to  give  some  more  of  these
hard  sayings  of  Bayazld,  because,  so  far  as  we  can  judge,
he  was  the  man  who  above  all  others  gave  to  Sufi
doctrine  its  permanent  shape.  The  Tazkirat  ul  Auliyd
records  this  saying  of  his,  “ God,  most  high,  has  laid  down
commands  and  prohibitions,  and  to  those  who  obey  Him
He  gives  a robe  of  honour.  Other  men  earnestly  strive
to  gain  that  robe,  but  I desire  of  Him  naught  but
Himself.”  Again,  “ Every  one  is  drowned  in  the  sea  of
works,  but  I am  drowned  in  the  sea  of  Tonification
( Tauliid ).  They  rely  on  their  own  righteousness,  but
I rely  on  His  free  bounty.”  Here  we  find  the  first
hint  of  an  esoteric  religion  for  “ interior  men  ” 1 {Ahl

1 ‘‘While  men  of  externals  believe  be  seen  through  a near  approach  to
that  there  is  nothing  in  existence  God,  and  a close  communion  with
but  what  is  visible  to  sight,  in-  His  omnipresent  Spirit.” — Fasusu’l

INTRODUCTION.


xviii

i biitin),  as  the  Sufis  called  themselves,  opposed  to  the
exoteric  religion  of  ordinary  Mussulmans.  The  latter
sought  salvation  through  the  works  of  the  law  {Shari’ at),
while  the  Sufis  followed  the  higher  road  of  the  truth

(ffaqiqat).

There  was  nothing  singular  or  exceptional  in  the  rise
of  an  esoteric  religion  in  Islam.  The  same  phenomenon
had  been  seen  in  Hinduism,  when  the  theosophy  of  the
Upanishads  and  the  Vedanta  was  engrafted  upon  the
older  Hindu  scriptures.  And  the  same  thing  happened
when  the  more  cultivated  Athenians  established  the
Eleusinian  mysteries,  which  aimed  at  presenting  to  the
initiated  or  “ mystics,”  as  they  were  called,  more  ade-
quate conceptions  of  Deity  than  those  of  the  current
popular  religion.

Bayazld  is  reported  to  have  said,  “ I saw  ‘ The  Truth,’
most  high  {Al  Haqq),  in  a dream.  He  said  to  me,  ‘ What
dost  thou  desire  ? ’ I answered,  ‘ I desire  what  Thou
desirest.’  He  said,  ‘ I am  to  thee  as  thou  to  Me.’  ”
Another  of  his  sayings  was  this,  “ I beheld  in  a dream
‘ The  Truth,’  most  high,  and  I asked,  ‘ What  is  the  way
to  Thee  ? ’ And  He  said,  ' Quit  thyself,  and  thou  wilt
attain  to  Me.’  ”

Al  Haqq,  or  “ The  Truth,”  the  name  here  used  by
Bayazld,  is  one  of  the  ninety-nine  names  of  Allah,  com-
monly recited  as  ejaculations  (Zilcr).  But  in  most  Sufi
writings  it  is  used  in  preference  to  all  the  rest,  presum-
ably because  it  indicates  a somewhat  wider  conception
of  Deity  than  the  “ Allah  ” of  the  Koran.  It  has  been
compared  to  the  “ Sat  ” of  the  Upanishads  ( Das  s&iende ),
but  it  may  well  be  doubted  if  the  Sufi  conception  of
deity  ever  became  quite  so  abstract  and  devoid  of  per-
sonal attributes  as  the  Brahma  of  the  Upanishads
and  the  Vedanta.1  With  the  Sufis  “ The  Truth  ” is  a


1 Thus  “The  Truth’’  seems  to  jaldl),  which  are,  in  fact,  the  good-
have  always  retained  the  attributes  ness  and  severity,  the  mercv  and
of  beauty  and  terror  (jamal  and  vengeance  of  the  Allah  of  the  Koran.


INTRODUCTION.


xix


personality  so  wide  as  to  include  in  itself  all  existence  and
all  action,  all  matter  and  all  force,  but  it  seems  to  be
regarded  as  a personality  for  all  that.  It  is  not  difficult
to  understand  how  this  idea  of  the  all-absorbing  and
all-embracing  nature  of  the  Deity  grew  up.

As  Deutsch  and  Renan  point  out,  the  Koran  does  not
clearly  determine  the  question  as  to  how  far  men  are
free  agents,  acting  on  their  own  initiative,  and  how  far
their  action  is  constrained  by  Allah.  In  fact,  it  speaks
in  merely  popular  language,  sometimes  ascribing  all
action  to  Allah,  and  sometimes  to  men's  own  agency.1
But  the  Scholastic  theologians,  or  Mutakallamln,  were
not  content  to  leave  the  question  in  this  prudent
obscurity.  By  reasonings  similar  to  those  of  St.  Augus-
tine and  Calvin,  they  demonstrated  that  Allah  was  the
only  real  agent  (Fail  i haqiql),  and  that  men  only  acted
under  the  “ compulsion  ” ( jabr ) of  the  divine  will.  This
extreme  view  seemed  to  glorify  Allah,  and  exalt  the  idea
of  the  divine  nature,  and  was  warmly  adopted  by  thorough-
going Sufis  like  Bayazid.  Thus  we  find  him  saying,  “ If
to-morrow  in  His  court  He  should  ask  me,  ' Why  hast
thou  not  done  so  and  so  ? ’ I should  prefer  that  to  His
asking,  ‘ "Why  hast  thou  done  so  and  so  ? ’ for  whatever
I do  of  myself  is  egotism,  and  egotism  and  ‘ I ’ are
‘ Shurk,’  or  ascribing  partners  to  God,  which  is  the
deadliest  of  sins.”  A story  is  told  of  a zealous  Sufi,
called  “ Muhasibi,”  from  his  habit  of  rigorous  self-exami-
nation, who  refused  to  inherit  from  his  father  because
the  father  had  held  the  doctrine  of  man’s  free  will,  and
was  therefore  a blasphemer  of  the  divine  omnipotence.
Shabistarl,  in  the  Gulshan  i Rciz,  lays  down  in  like
manner  that  all  who  hold  any  doctrine  other  than
“ compulsion  ” (jabr),  are  no  better  than  Magian  dualists.
It  may  be  remarked  by  the  way  that  St.  Augustine,
like  Bayazid,  had  once  held  the  dualistic  view.  Was

it  a similar  revulsion  from  this  view  which  im-

When  Bayazid  had  become  convinced  that  all  the
action  in  the  universe  is  the  direct  action  of  “ The
Truth,”  he  soon  came  to  the  further  conclusion  that
there  is  no  real  existence  in  the  universe  save  the  exist-
ence of  the  same  “ Truth.”  By  the  aid  of  allegorical
interpretation,  that  wonderful  instrument  for  putting
new  wine  into  old  bottles,  it  was  easy  to  deduce  this
doctrine  from  various  texts  of  the  Koran,  e.g.  “ To  Him
we  shall  return,”  “ All  creatures  shall  pass  away  except
the  face  of  thy  Lord,”  &c.  Consequently  we  find
Bayazid  claiming  actual  identity  with  the  Deity.  He
said,  “ My  similitude  is  as  the  ocean,  for  its  depth  is  not
seen,  neither  its  beginning  or  ending.”  One  asked  him,
“ What  is  the  empyrean  (’ arsh ) ? ” He  answered,  “ It
is  I.”  “ What  is  the  throne  of  Allah  ? ” “ It  is  I.”

“ What  are  the  pen  and  the  tablet  ? ” He  said,  “ I am
the  pen  and  the  tablet.”  They  said,  “ Before  God,  most
high,  Abraham,  Moses,  and  Muhammad  (on  whom  be
peace  and  blessing),  are  very  slaves.”  He  made  answer,
“ All  these  am  I.”  They  said,  “ Before  God,  most  high,
Gabriel,  Michael,  Israfil,  and  ’Azrall  (on  whom  be  peace),
are  very  slaves.”  He  said,  “ I am  all  these.”  His  ques-
tioners were  silent.  Bayazid  added,  “ Of  a surety  he
who  is  annihilated  ( mahv ) in  ‘ The  Truth  ’ attains  to  the
very  reality  of  all  that  exists.” 1 To  use  the  words  of
the  German  mystic,  Eckart,  he  “ breaks  through  to  the
Oneness,”  and  abides  in  “ the  ground  of  his  soul.” 2 This
is  the  doctrine  expressed  in  the  well-known  Sufi  formula,
“ Bciqa  ha’ cl  id  fana,”  or  abiding  in  “ The  Truth  ” after  anni-
hilating self.

Sufis  was  that  of  Junaid  of  Bagdad,  who,  according  to
Jaini  in  the  Nafahatu-l-Uns , died  in  297  A.H.  It
is  said  that  the  mantle  of  ’Ali  had  descended  to
Junaid  through  Hasan  of  Basra,  as  an  outward  and
visible  sign  of  his  apostolic  succession.  One  of  his
sayings  was  this,  “ The  hearts  of  religious  men  are
veiled  by  three  veils ; to  wit,  estimation  of  their  own
works,  expectation  of  recompense  for  them,  and  pre-
occupation with  the  hope  of  divine  favours.”  They
asked  Junaid,  “ When  is  a servant  a true  servant  in
his  devotion  ? ” He  answered,  “ When  he  sees  all
things  under  the  absolute  sway  of  God,  most  high,
and  that  all  come  from  God,  and  subsist  in  God,  and
return  to  God,  as  God  hath  said,  ‘ Glory  be  to  Him  in
whose  hand  is  sway  over  all  things,  and  to  Him  shall
ye  return’”  (Sura,  xxxvi.  83).

Husain  bin  Mansur  A1  Hallaj  (“  the  wool-carder  ”)
was  a disciple  of  Junaid.  His  life  is  given  by  Ibn
Khalliqan,  ’Attar,  Jam!,  and  others.  He  was  put  to
death  at  Bagdad  in  309  a.h.  for  saying,  “I  am  ‘The
Truth  ’ :’  ( And  ul  Haqq).  Imam  Ghazall  excuses  him  on
the  ground  that  he  spoke  in  this  way  out  of  excessive
love  to  God,  and  that  such  expressions  may  be  likened
to  the  following:  “ I am  he  whom  I love,  and  he  whom
I love  is  I.  We  are  as  two  souls  in  one  body.  When
ye  see  me  ye  see  him,  and  when  ye  see  him  ye  see
me.”  But  in  another  passage  quoted  in  Pococke’s
Specimen  Historic  Arabum,  p.  263,  he  condemns  the
use  of  all  such  expressions.  When  the  sentence  of
death  was  passed,  Hallaj  said,  “ You  cannot  scourge  me
or  shed  my  blood ; my  belief  is  Islam  based  on  the
Sunna ; I acknowledge  the  pre-eminence  of  the  Imams,
the  well-directed  Khalifs  ( i.e . the  first  four  Khalifs),  and
of  the  rest  of  the  Companions,  may  the  blessing  of  God
be  upon  them ; on  God  do  I call  that  He  protect  my
blood.”  Shortly  before  he  died,  he  cried  out,  “ My
friend  is  not  guilty  of  injuring  me ; he  gives  me  to

drink  what,  as  master  of  the  feast,  he  drinks  himself.
When  he  passes  me  the  cup  he  invites  me  to  taste

a great  flood  occurred,  they  said  it  had  been  caused  by
the  ashes  of  llallaj,  which  had  been  flung  into  the
Tigris.  Others  averred  that  it  was  not  he  who  had
been  put  to  death,  but  one  of  his  persecutors  who  had
been  made  to  resemble  him,  and  had  thus  suffered  in
his  stead.

The  following  are  some  of  his  sayings : “ The  way
to  God  is  two  steps,  one  step  out  of  this  world,  and
one  step  out  of  the-  next  world,  and  lo ! you  are  there
with  the  Lord  ( Maula ).”  They  asked  him  of  poverty,

and  he  said,  “ The  poor  man  ( Faqlr ) is  he  who  dispenses
with  what  is  beside  God,  and  looks  to  Him  alone.”
Again  he  said,  “ The  true  ecstatic  Sufi  is  he  who  knows
One  person,  and  One  person  knows  him.”  Again,
“ Whoso  worships  God  by  the  light  of  ordinary  religion
is  as  one  who  seeks  the  sun  by  the  light  of  the  stars.”
When  he  said,  “ I am  ‘ The  Truth,’  ” they  said  to  him,
“ Say  Re  is  ‘ The  Truth.’  ” He  replied,  “ Verily,  He  is  all
in  all,  and  yet  ye  say  He  is  lost  (in  me).  Nay,  Husain
is  lost  (in  Him).  The  circumfluent  ocean  is  not  lost
{gum),  and  never  decreases.”  A saying  was  current
that  Hallaj  was  put  to  death  because  he  revealed
secrets.

The  next  leading  Sufi  teacher  was  Abu  Sa’id  bin
Abu-l-Khair  of  Malina  in  Khorasan,  who  lived  from
396  to  440  a.h.1  In  early  youth  he  felt  drawn  to  a
religious  life,  and  became  the  disciple  of  Abu-1-Fazl

Luqman  of  Sarakhs,  by  whom  he  was  admitted  into
one  of  the  Darvesh  orders.1  At  Sarakhs  he  passed,
it  is  said,  seven  years,  practising  the  most  rigorous
asceticism.  He  would  spend  hours  calling  out,  “Allah,
Allah ! ” just  as  St.  Francis  at  the  outset  of  his  career
used  to  confine  his  devotions  to  the  ejaculation,  “ My
God,  my  God ! The  following  sayings  of  his  are  re-
corded in  the  Haft  Iqlim.  When  asked  what  a Sufi  was,
he  said,  “ Whatever  is  in  thy  head  forget  it,  whatever
is  in  thy  hand  give  it  away,  and  whatever  happens  to
thee  disregard  it.”  Again,  “ The  more  a man  knows
of  the  world  the  less  he  knows  of  God.”  He  said,
" Love  is  God’s  snare,”  i.e.  the  feeling  of  attraction  to
God  is  implanted  in  the  heart  in  order  to  draw  man
to  God.  After  conversing  with  the  great  philosopher,
Ibn  Sina  or  Avicena,  he  said,  “ I see  (by  spiritual  in-
tuition) all  that  he  knows.”  That  is  to  say,  he  claimed
to  be  illuminated  by  the  “ inner  light,”  which  plays  so
important  a part  in  all  mystic  systems.  Thus  Mystical
theology  is  described  by  a very  high  authority 2 as  a
“ pure  knowledge  of  God  which  the  soul  ordinarily
receives  in  a luminous  darkness  or  obscure  light  of
sublime  contemplation,  together  with  an  experimental
love  so  intimate  that  the  soul  losing  itself  altogether
is  united  to  God  and  transformed  into  Him.”  How  to
describe  these  supra  - sensible  sensations  has  always
been  a difficulty  for  mystics.  Jeremy  Taylor  says,
“ When  they  suffer  transportation  beyond  the  burden
and  support  of  reason,  they  feel  they  know  not  what,
and  call  it  what  they  please.”  Among  the  Sufis  Abu-1-
Khair  seems  to  have  been  the  first  to  use  that  sym-
bolical language  which  characterises  all  subsequent  Sufi
writings.  This  language  is  explained  in  the  Gulshan-i-
Haz,  which  was  written  about  1317  a.d.,  corresponding

to  71 7 a.h.  Wine  is  the  symbol  of  spiritual  transports,
the  eye  of  the  divine  severity,  the  lip  of  the  divine
loving-kindness,  the  mole  of  the  divine  unity,  and  so

St.  Bernard  of  Clairvaux  extracted  from  the  Canticles,
only  much  more  elaborate.  Later  Persian  poets  like

it  in  equivocal  senses,  and  this  has  led  some  writers  to
represent  Sufi  doctrine  as  mere  lawless  pleasure-wor-
ship, and  Sufi  poetry  as  a mere  counterpart  of  the
poems  of  Anacreon  and  Moore.  But  this  view  of  Sufi
doctrine  is  hardly  borne  out  by  the  evidence.

As  Abu-l-Ivhair  gave  the  Sufis  their  poetic  symbolism,
so  Imam  Ghazall  of  Tus  in  Khorasan,  who  also  lived  in
the  fifth  century  of  the  Hijira,  gave  them  their  meta-
physical terminology.  He  was  a man  of  great  learning,
at  once  a theologian  of  the  highest  repute  and  a philo-
sopher versed  in  Greek  metaphysics,  as  interpreted  by  his
predecessors  A1  EarabI  and  the  celebrated  Ibn  Slna  or
Avicena.  He  had  written  a book  on  the  “ Vivification
of  the  Sciences  ” ( Ihlya  ul  ’ulum),  of  which  it  was  said,
that  were  all  other  records  of  Islam  to  be  lost,  the  entire
religion  might  be  recovered  from  this  book.  To  judge
from  his  treatise,  “ The  Deliverer  from  Error  ” ( Munqiz
min  az-zalcil),1  he  seems  to  have  been  a thinker  something
like  Schelling,  who  tried  to  find  a sure  basis  of  know-
ledge in  an  “ intellectual  intuition  ” transcending  the
ordinary  understanding.  And,  to  complete  the  parallel,
just  as  Schelling’s  philosophical  mysticism  was  disposed
of  by  Sir  W.  Hamilton,  Dean  Mansel,  and  Mr.  Herbert
Spencer,  so  Ghazall  eventually  found  a refuter  in  the
celebrated  Ibn  Roshd,  better  known  as  Averroes.  How-
ever, it  is  not  with  Ghazali’s  attempt  to  make  philosophy
mystical  that  we  are  here  concerned,  but  with  his  attempt

to  mnke  mysticism  philosophical  by  furnishing  it  with
a philosophical  terminology.  And  this  he  did  by  appro-
priating the  terminology  of  the  Alexandrian  Neoplatonists.
The  Dabistan  in  its  chapter  on  “ The  religion  of  the  philo-
sophers,” states  that  there  are  two  classes  of  philosophers,
the  Orientals  ( Shardqln  or  Mushraqln),  who  included  all
down  to  Plato,  and  the  Occidentals ; and  elsewhere  it
states  that  the  belief  of  the  Sufis  is  the  same  as  that
of  the  Platonists.  Moreover,  Haji  Khalfa  in  his  article
on  Sufism,  states  that  the  Sufis  borrowed  their  termin-
ology from  the  Oriental  class  of  philosophers,  as  will  be
manifest  to  any  one  who  examines  their  books.1  There
can  be  no  doubt  as  to  the  correctness  of  these  statements.
In  reading  Sufi  books  we  are  constantly  confronted  from
this  time  forward  with  the  technical  terms  of  Greek,
and  especially  Neoplatonist  philosophy.” 2 And  the  use
of  this  new  terminology  undoubtedly  modified  to  some
extent  the  older  Sufi  theosophy.  To  give  only  two
instances  of  this  : — First,  “ The  Truth  ” was  assimilated
to  the  Deity  of  Plotinus.3  “ The  Truth  ” no  longer  acts
like  Allah  by  willing,  but  by  being.  Creation  is  not
effected  by  a fiat,  but  by  emanation  from  the  Divine
Being,  which  rays  itself  out  into  creatures  by  “ proodos”
as  Plotinus  called  it.  The  Logos  (’ Aql  i kull)  first
proceeds  from  the  Deity,  then  the  Spirit  (JSrafs  i
kull)  from  the  Logos,  and  so  on.  And  ultimately  all
creatures  are  reabsorbed  into  the  Deity  by  a process
analogous  to  the  “ epistrophe  ” of  Plotinus.  Secondly,
in  place  of  the  older  vague  statements  about  the  “ inner
light,”  we  get  almost  the  precise  doctrine  of  Plotinus  as

This  Plotinian  doctrine  is  well  set  out  in  a passage  in
Barclay’s  “ Apology  for  the  True  Christian  Divinity  as
the  same  is  held  forth  and  preached  by  the  people  called
in  scorn  Quakers”  (edition  of  1678,  p.  126).  “Yea,
there  is  a book  translated  out  of  the  Arabic,  which  gives
an  account  of  one  Hai  Ebn  Yokdan,  who,  without  con-
verse of  men,  living  in  an  island  alone,  attained  to  such
a profound  knowledge  of  God  as  to  have  immediate
converse  with  Him,  and  to  affirm  that  the  best  and
most  certain  knowledge  of  God  is  not  that  which  is
attained  by  premisses  premised,  and  conclusions  deduced,
but  that  which  is  enjoyed  by  conjunction  of  the  mind
•of  man  with  the  Supreme  Intellect,  after  the  mind  is
purified  from  its  corruptions,  and  is  separated  from  all
bodily  images,  and  is  gathered  into  a perfect  stillness.”  1

After  the  end  of  the  fifth  century  of  the  Hijira,
nothing  of  consequence  was  added  to  the  doctrine  of  the
Sufis,  though,  as  Haji  Khalfa  remarks,  very  many  books
were  written  about  it.  Nearly  all  the  chief  poets  of
Persia  either  were  or  professed  to  be  Sufis,  and  even
works  on  ethics  like  the  Akhlaq-i-Jaldll  use  the  Sufi
phraseology  whenever  it  is  possible.

The  poems  which  are  regarded  as  the  best  expositions
of  Sufi  doctrine  are  the  following : —

1.  The  Iictdiqa  ul  Haqiqat,  or  “Garden  of  Verity,”
•composed  by  Hakim  Sana!  in  535  a.h.

2.  The  Ildhlndma,  or  “ Divine  Book,”  and  the  Mantiq
ut  Tear,  or  “ Discourse  of  the  Birds,”  composed  by  Farldu-
d-Dln  ’Attar  about  the  end  of  the  sixth  century  of  the
Hijira.  The  latter  has  been  published  together  with  a
French  translation  by  M.  Garcin  de  Tassy.  He  lias  also
published  a full  abstract  in  his  “ Poiisie  philosophique  et
religieuse  chez  les  Persans.”

3.  The  Masncivi  of  Jalalu-’d-Dln  Rum!,  which  was

mentioned,  but  it  is  a very  useful  compendium  of  Sufi
doctrine.  The  text  has  been  published  together  with  a
translation  and  notes  by  Triibner,  1880.


III.

GENEltAL  VIEW  OF  THE  MASNAVI.

The  poem  begins  with  what  the  late  Professor  Palmer
called  “ The  song  of  the  reed,”  figuring  the  love  of  the
soul  to  God  and  its  longing  to  be  reunited  with  God.
This  is  the  keynote  of  the  whole  poem,  which,  if  we
may  parody  Aristotle’s  definition  of  tragedy,  may  be
defined  as  an  attempt  to  purify  the  religious  senti-
ment by  love.  By  “ love  ” the  poet  seems  to  mean,  in
the  first  place,  such  a longing  for  God  as  that  de-
scribed in  the  Psalm,  “ Like  as  the  hart  desireth  the
waterbrooks,  so  longeth  my  soul  after  Thee,  0 God.”
And,  in  the  second  place,  Ruml's  conception  of  love
certainly  included  some  part  of  what  St.  John  and
St.  Paul  meant  by  love  {Agape).  It  certainly  included
mutual  charity,  at  any  rate  as  far  as  the  brethren  of
the  order  and  other  “ friends  of  God  ” were  concerned,
though  of  course  it  did  not  amount  to  that  universal
charity,  that  “ enthusiasm  of  humanity,”  which  possessed

1 It  is  styled  the  “ Amulet  of  the  Malcolm  in  his  “ History  of  Persia,”
Soul”  by  Khaja  'Aini,  one  of  the  ii.  280,  calls  it  one  of  the  “ Scriptures
Constantinople  ’ llama  ; and  Sir  J.  of  Persia.”


INTRODUCTION.


xxviii

St.  Francis,  and  impelled  him  to  spend  and  be  spent  in
the  service  of  the  poor  and  outcast.

The  claim  of  the  Sufis  to  feel  love  to  God  seems  to
strike  some  critics  as  absurd.  No  doubt  Moslems  who
only  regard  Christ  as  a great  prophet  cannot  feel
the  same  motives  for  love  of  God  as  those  felt  by
Christians,  hut  there  is  no  absurdity  in  supposing  that
they  may  feel  all  the  motives  which  operated  on  Old
Testament  writers.  The  Allah  of  the  Koran  is  precisely
the  Jehovah  of  the  Old  Testament,  a divine  King  of
kings  dwelling  in  the  highest  heaven  (’ ctrsJi ),  a God  of
goodness  and  of  severity,  of  mercy  and  of  vengeance,
who  rules  the  world  with  almighty  and  irresistible
power.  Love  is  excited  by  goodness,  not  by  power,
and  thus  we  find  pious  Moslems  always  dwelling  by
preference  on  the  merciful  attributes  of  the  Deity ; thus,
for  instance,  in  the  common  formula  the  “ Bismillah,”
they  always  address  Allah  as  “ the  Merciful,  the  Com-
passionate.” And  it  is  precisely  this  aspect  of  the
divine  nature  which  is  insisted  on  by  Eumi  as  the
basis  for  his  principle  of  love.  Throughout  the  Masnavi
his  main  object  is  to  bring  out  the  “ fatherhood  of  God  ”
and  to  explain  away  His  terrible  attributes,  the  com-
pulsion or  predestination,  the  origin  of  evil,  and  all  the
seeming  anomalies  in  the  divine  government  which  per-
plexed Job,  the  Ecclesiast  and  many  later  theologians.
He  approaches  all  these  questions  not  on  their  meta-
physical but  on  their  moral  side,  that  is,  through  his
principle  of  love.  The  more  a man  loves,  the  deeper
he  penetrates  the  divine  purposes.  Love  is  the  “ astro-
labe of  heavenly  mysteries,”  the  “ eye-salve  ” which
clears  the  spiritual  eye  and  makes  it  clairvoyant.  He
compares  it  to  the  love  of  an  affectionate  child  which
divines  the  reasons  for  its  father’s  severity,  and  to  the
love  of  a lover  who  finds  excuses  for  the  cruelty  of  his
mistress  ; in  fact,  he  explains  it  by  the  analogy  of  earthly
love.


INTRODUCTION.


XXIX


It  is  this  perilous  analogy  which  leads  him,  as  it
has  also  led  many  Christian  mystics,  into  some  of  his
worst  errors.  That  fervid  Copt,  Origen,  was  perhaps
the  first  to  apply  the  phraseology  of  human  love  to  the
love  of  God.  Like  his  successor  St.  Bernard,  he  did  this
by  his  allegorical  interpretation  of  the  Canticles,  a book
which  it  is  said  the  Eabbis  were  half-inclined  to  exclude
from  the  Canon.  And  the  line  of  sentiment  thus  started
has  been  followed  by  later  mystics,  very  much  to  their
own  detriment  and  discredit.  As  Kingsley  points  out,1
it  tends  to  produce  a kind  of  hysterical  devotion,  con-
sisting in  transports  and  raptures,  which  are  certainly
very  alien  from  sober  and  sensible  piety.  It  may  be
said  for  Eumi  in  the  first  place,  that  he  is  not  so  great
a sinner  in  this  respect  as  many  others  of  the  mystic
fraternity.  Secondly,  he  is  careful  to  point  out  in
language  which  would  almost  satisfy  Dean  Mansel  and
Mr.  Herbert  Spencer,  that  man  is  utterly  unable  to  form
adequate  conceptions  of  the  Absolute,  and  that  all
symbols  of  it  derived  from  the  world  of  sense  are  more
or  less  misleading,  though  he  justifies  their  use  by  the
example  of  the  Koran  and  by  the  necessities  of  human
thought.  Lastly,  he  was  no  doubt  influenced  by  Platonist
doctrine.  Plato  held  that  the  soul  in  its  ante-natal
condition  had  been  conversant  with  the  “ ideas  ” (or
forms)  of  heavenly  goodness  and  beauty,  and  that  in
its  sojourn  on  earth  it  is  reminded  of  these  ideas  by  the
sight  of  beautiful  things.  Beauty,  it  has  been  said,
“ stands  on  the  threshold  of  the  mystical  world  ” and
rekindles  the  love  of  the  soul  for  its  heavenly  prototype.
If  Christian  poets,  like  Wordsworth  in  his  “ Ode  on
Immortality  ” and  Newman  when  he  talks  of  “ angel
faces  loved  long  since  and  lost  awhile,”  might  without
reproach  use  part  of  Plato’s  imagery,  we  must  not  be
too  hard  on  Eumi  for  travelling  a little  further  in  the
same  direction.  On  the  whole,  then,  it  does  not  seem

1 Speaking  of  St.  Francis  de  Sales  in  a note  to  the  “ Saint’s  Tragedy.”


XXX


INTRODUCTION.


fair  to  question  the  sincerity  or  genuineness  of  the  love
to  God  which  Eumi  professed  to  feel,  though  much  of
his  phraseology  must  seem  offensive  and  unseemly  to
modern  readers.

The  way  in  which  he  applies  his  principle  of  love
may  be  seen  in  the  following  instances  of  its  application
to  the  questions  of  the  origin  of  evil,  “ compulsion,”  the
grounds  of  belief,  and  the  mystical  journey  to  “ Union.”

First  as  regards  the  mystery  of  evil,  Eumi  says  a skilful
painter  is  of  necessity  able  to  paint  ugly  pictures  as  well
as  fair  ones.  Hence  the  “ knower  ” does  not,  like  the
Magians,  set  up  over  against  the  good  Ormuzd  an  evil
Ahriman  to  account  for  the  origin  of  evil,  but  recognises
that  God  created  all  things,  even  including  what  seems
to  us  evil.  But  what  we  call  evil  has  in  reality  no  real
existence  of  its  own,  being  merely,  as  St.  Augustine  said,
a “ negation,”  or  not-being — a departure  from  the  Only
Self-existent  Being.  Here  the  poet  first  takes  “ not-
being  ” in  its  literal  sense  of  “ nothing,”  and  then,  misled
by  the  old  notion  that  all  words  must  have  things  an-
swering to  them,1  as  something, — as  nothing  in  relation
to  God,  but  as  a very  pernicious  something  in  relation
to  man.  In  the  next  place,  the  poet  points  out  that  much
of  what  we  call  evil  is  only  relative, — what  is  evil  for  one
being  good  for  another, — nay  more,  that  evil  itself  is  often
turned  into  good  for  the  good.  Further,  he  insists  on  the
probationary  design  of  much  so-called  evil.  As  Bishop
Butler  says,  life  is  a state  of  probation,  and  probation
involves  the  existence  of  evil  lusts  and  pains  to  prove  us.
How,  the  poet  asks,  could  there  be  self-control  without
evil  passions  to  be  controlled,  or  patience  without  the
pressure  of  afflictions  to  be  borne  ? Much  evil,  again,  is
medicinal, — the  jail  serves  as  the  mosque  of  the  criminal,
because  it  makes  him  cry  to  God,  just  as  fasting  and
discipline  are  the  school  of  the  ill-regulated  passions.
Lastly,  much  evil  has  a punitive  purpose.  “ He  who
1 See  p.  52.


INTRODUCTION.


XXXI


grieves  the  Lotjos  must  look  for  tribulation  in  the
world.”

So,  again,  with  the  question  of  “compulsion”  or  pre-
destination:— God  is  the  "Only  Ileal  Agent,”  and  in
His  inscrutable  wisdom  He  undoubtedly  fashions  some  to
be  vessels  of  wrath,  such  as  were  Iblis,  Cain,  Nimrod,
Pharaoh,1  and  Abu  Jahl ; but,  as  we  can  see  only  a small
part  of  the  divine  scheme,  we  must  be  wary  of  judging  in
these  cases.  Adam  was  rebuked  for  scorning  Iblis,  and
it  may  be  that  Iblis  will  one  day  be  turned  again  into  an
angel  of  light.  So  it  is  said  that  Pharaoh  was  a doer
of  God’s  will  equally  with  Moses,  and  was  wont  at  night
to  bewail  the  “ compulsion  ” which  made  him  oppose
that  faithful  servant  of  God.  But  the  “ knower,”  while
refusing  to  judge  others,  will  not  be  backward  in  judging
himself.  He  will  admit  the  ability  to  choose  good  and
refuse  evil,  which  his  own  consciousness  testifies  to  as
existing  in  himself.  He  will  not,  like  Iblis,  throw  the
responsibility  for  his  sins  upon  the  Creator,  but  rather
he  will  cry  with  Adam,  “ O Lord,  we  have  blackened  our
own  faces ! ” Finally,  when  man’s  will  is  altogether
identified  with  God’s  will,  and  be  finds  his  chief  plea-
sure in  doing  God’s  will,  the  whole  opposition  and  dis-
tinction between  “compulsion”  and  free  will  is  annihilated,
just  as  raindrops  falling  into  oyster-shells  become  pearls.

Next  as  to  the  grounds  of  belief  and  evidences  of
religion: — There  are  some  minds,  illuminated  by  the
“ dry  light  ” of  reason,  which  are  irresistibly  impelled
to  prove  all  things.  Such  a sober  and  candid  mind,
for  instance,  as  that  of  Bishop  Butler  cannot  rest  in
what  seems  to  it  mere  “ enthusiasm.”  Bishop  Butler,
like  his  master  Locke,  cannot  help  proportioning  his
beliefs  to  the  evidence  for  them.  Even  for  the  glory  of
God  he  cannot  exaggerate  the  evidence,  and,  where  he
finds  only  probable  evidence,  he  will  not  pretend  to
demonstrable  certitude.  And  it  may  be  noted  by  the

1 See  “ Dabistan,”  by  Shea  and  Troyer,  iii.  272,  note.


XXXU


INTRODUCTION.


way,  that  our  poet  himself  does  not  disdain  to  use  the
argument  from  probability  in  addressing  the  worldly,
though  he  insists  that  his  own  faith  rests  on  far  deeper
foundations.  In  addressing  the  worldly,  however,  he
says,  “ Even  in  your  trade  you  act  on  probability.  You
despatch  goods  by  sea  at  the  risk  of  shipwreck,  for  if
you  decline  the  risk  you  can  gain  no  livelihood.  And
so  you  ought  to  act  with  the  great  venture  of  faith.”
But  for  himself  the  poet  cannot  rest  on  mere  probability.
He  must  have  “indefectible  certitude,” — a firm  convic-
tion independent  of  varying  moods  and  shifting  proba-
bilities,— a very  “ passion  of  belief.”  A true  lover,  he  says,
is  ashamed  to  demand  proofs  of  his  mistress,  and  prides
himself  on  trusting  her  in  spite  of  appearances  teding
against  her.1  He  has  an  evidence  in  his  heart  which
makes  him  turn  away  from  all  external  evidences  with
disgust.  This  was  just  the  feeling  with  which  the
Evangelical  School  regarded  the  “ Probabilities  ” of  Butler
and  the  “ Evidences  ” of  Paley.  Similarly  we  read  in
Cardinal  Newman's  “Apologia”"  that  Mr.  Keble  “ascribed
the  firmness  of  assent  which  we  give  to  religious  doc-
trine, not  to  the  probabilities  which  introduced  it,  but
to  the  living  power  of  faith  and  love  which  accepted
it.”  Just  so  the  Masnavi  teaches  that  it  is  love  which
generates  intuition  and  belief.  Love  absorbs  a man’s
whole  being  and  makes  him  indifferent  to  the  cavils  of
cold  reason ; just  as  Zaid,  smarting  with  the  pain  of  the
blow,  was  unable  to  attend  to  the  hair-splitting  distinc-
tions of  his  assailant.

Not  only  is  faith  generated  by  love,  but,  what  is  more,
faith  generated  by  any  other  motive  is  worthless.  Faith,
like  that  of  respectable  conformists,  growing  from  mere
blind  imitation  and  the  contagion  of  custom,  or  like
that  of  scholastic  theologians,  consisting  in  mere  intel-
lectual apprehension  of  orthodox  dogmas  (Jama’at),  and
all  mere  mechanical  and  routine  professions  of  belief, —
1 Credo  quia  impossible.  8 “ Apologia,”  p.  19.


INTRODUCTION.


xxxiii

summed  lip  by  the  poet  under  the  general  name  of  the
“ yoke  of  custom  ” ( [taqlld ) — profit  nothing.  They  only
produce  the  spiritual  torpor  called  by  Dante  “ nccidia."
To  be  of  any  value,  faith  must  be  rooted  and  grounded  in
love.  The  mere  external  righteousness  generated  by
“ taqlld,” — the  mere  matter-of-course  adoption  of  the
virtues  of  the  age,  the  class,  the  sect, — is  compared  to  a
“ veil  of  light  ” (formal  righteousness)  which  hides  the
Truth  more  entirely  than  the  “ veil  of  darkness  ” (open
sin).  For  “ self-deluding  goodness  is  of  necessity  un-
repentant,” while  the  avowed  sinner  is  already  self-
condemned,  and  so  advanced  one  step  on  the  road  to
repentance.

Since  there  are  so  many  kinds  of  spurious  faith  current
in  the  world,  the  pious  man  must  walk  warily,  and  before
copying  the  words  and  actions  of  those  who  seem  to  be
religious,  he  must  test  and  prove  them.  Sometimes  he
will  find  their  professions  contradicted  by  their  practice  ;
sometimes,  especially  in  moments  of  unguarded  anger,
their  “ speech  will  betray  them,”  and  show’  their  motive
to  be  odium  tlieologicum  and  not  religious  love.  He  must
apply  a sort  of  Socratic  clenckus  to  their  shibboleths,  and
see  if  the  root  of  the  matter  be  in  them.  Even  when  he
has  found  a genuine  saint,  he  must  not  run  away  with
the  notion  that  all  he  has  to  do  is  to  copy  his  outward
conduct,  but  he  must  try  to  divine  his  inward  motives
and  ruling  principles,  and  strive  earnestly  to  attain  the
disciplined  affections,  the  concentrated  will,  the  all-
absorbing  love  of  God,  which  are  the  characteristics  of
His  true  children.

God  judges  not  as  men  judge,  from  outward  conduct,
but  looks  at  the  heart,  the  secret  motives,  and  the  “ aspi-
ration.”1 Hence  the  poet  holds  outward  forms  and  rites
to  be  matters  of  very  minor  importance.  Islam  is  the
“religion  of  Abraham”2  ( minus  the  priesthood) — a religion

1 See  Book  VI. — Prologue.

- See  Koran  iii.  89,  and  Deutsch,  “Remains,”  p.  129.


XXXIV


INTRODUCTION.


of  new  moons  and  fasts,  of  circumcision,  of  purifications,
of  holy  war,1  of  precise  postures  in  prayer,  which,  as  the
late  Dr.  Liddon  pointed  out,  are  the  very  postures  de-
scribed in  the  Psalms.2  Naturally  Moslems  get  to  rely  on
these  forms  too  entirely, — to  lose  the  sense  of  proportion
in  religious  matters, — to  fancy  that  a painful  and  scrupu-
lous observance  of  forms  will  atone  for  the  neglect  of
the  weightier  matters  of  the  law, — the  essential  religious
graces  summed  up  by  St.  Paul  under  the  name  of  Charity.
Hence  the  poet  was  led  to  regard  forms,  generally  speak-
ing, as  indifferent.  In  the  parable  of  Moses  and  the
Shepherd  he  points  out  that  so  long  as  a worshipper  is
inspired  by  love  of  God  the  words  in  which  he  expresses
his  devotion  are  quite  immaterial.  He  says,  “ Fools  exalt
the  Mosque,  and  ignore  the  true  temple  in  the  heart.”
He  has  even  a good  word  for  idolaters  who  act  up  to
their  lights.  The  old  satire  of  Xenophanes,  that  if  lions
had  a god  they  would  represent  him  as  a lion,  would
affect  him  not  at  all.  He  says  it  is  not  from  any  per-
verse preference  that  men  buy  counterfeit  gold,  but  only
because  it  seems  to  them  genuine.  Man  is  not  saved  by
“ naming  the  divine  names  ” with  orthodox  accuracy,3  or
by  worshipping  with  “ fair  rites,”  but  by  heartfelt  love
and  earnest  endeavour  to  please  God.  In  every  nation
he  who  loves  God  and  does  His  will  according  to  his
lights  is  accepted  of  Him.

The  final  consummation  of  love  to  God  is  union  with
God  ( icasl ).  According  to  the  Sufi  gnosis  (ma'rifat),
God  is  the  only  Being  who  has  any  real  existence.
Everything  besides  God  is  “ not-being  ” (’adm),  which  is
sometimes  described  as  mere  negation  of  existence,  and
at  other  times  as  having  some  positive  evil  elements.
Man  is  a compound  of  these  two  ingredients,  namely,
real  divine  being  and  the  phenomenal  and  illusory

1 See  Deuteronomy,  chap.  xx.  2 Psalm  xcv.  6.

3 Jalalu-’d-Din  once  said  that  he  agreed  with  all  the  seventy-three

sects.  Nafahatu-’l  Uns,  p.  532.


INTRODUCTION.


XXXV


not-being.  The  saints  “ see  things  as  they  really  are."
They  recognise  the  divinity  within  them.  They  see  that
“ God  is  nearer  to  them  than  their  neck-vein.”  And,
kindled  with  love  of  this  divine  element  of  their  nature,
they  concentrate  all  their  endeavours  on  developing  it
and  realising  its  perfection.  This  is  effected  by  purging
away  the  clouds  of  not-being.  To  use  the  mystical
term,  they  “ascend  inwards”  (introrsum  ascendant).  I’>y
spiritual  meditation  and  a course  of  rigorous  asceticism
they  sweep  away  self-consciousness,  self-will,  and  all
human  passions  and  affections  which  separate  them  from
the  one  real  being.  And  thus  they  attain  the  annihila-
tion or  death  of  “self  " (Jana),  which  results  in  the
spiritual  resurrection  to  eternal  life  in  God  ( baqa ) ; in
other  words,  they  obtain  union  with  God,  being  entirely
absorbed  in  Him  and  abiding  for  ever  in  Him.

To  use  the  language  of  Catholic  theologians,  the
Masnavi  is  an  exposition  of  “experimental”  mysticism,
and  not  a treatise  of  “doctrinal”  mysticism.  Hence
liumi  does  not  set  out  all  this  Sufi  gnosis  wit  h the  logical
precision  of  a systematic  treatise,  like  the  Gulshan-i-raz,
but  rather  assumes  it  all  as  known  to  his  readers.  He
describes  it  all  in  the  language  of  emotion  and  imagina-
tion, rather  than  in  that  of  the  intellect.  Thus,  for
instance,  he  nowhere  describes  in  precise  terms  the
method  in  which  God  is  conceived  to  “ ray  Himself
out  ” into  the  visible  universe  by  the  emanations  of  the
Logos  ( aql-i-kull ),  the  Pneuma  ( nafs-i-hnll ),  and  the  rest.
He  only  introduces  these  terms  as  already  familiar  to
his  readers.  In  like  manner,  he  does  not  describe  with
minuteness  the  various  stages  of  the  Sufi  progress  up  to
union,  which  are  described  at  great  length,  and,  it  may
be  added,  with  great  discrepancy,  by  other  Sufi  writers.

European  critics  generally  condemn  this  central  Sufi
doctrine.  Tholuck  in  his  “ Ssufismus  ” calls  it  “ a pan-
theistic theosophy,”  but  in  his  later  “ Bliithensammlung  ”
he  traces  some  analogies  between  it  and  certain  Christian


XXXVI


INTRODUCTION.


views.  Garcin  de  Tassy  calls  it  a “ funeste  doctrine,”
assuming  that  it  implies  the  indifference  of  good  and
evil,  and  the  negation  of  all  morality.  But  as  regards
Iiumi  and  the  better  Sufi  writers,  this  criticism  seems
hardly  fair.  They  may  possibly  be  branded  as  incon-
sequent logicians,  but  they  cannot  justly  be  condemned
as  bad  moralists,  and  charged  with  holding  conclusions
which  they  did  not  draw,  but  distinctly  repudiated.1  In
one  respect,  however,  their  doctrine  of  the  ultimate
absorption  of  man  into  God  does  offend  a very  wide-
spread and  natural  human  sentiment,  namely,  the  in-
stinctive hope  of  seeing  again  those  whom  we  have  loved
in  this  life.  The  Muhammadan  paradise,  which  is,  in
fact,  the  temporal  Messianic  kingdom  of  the  Jewish
prophets  with  some  embellishments  probably  suggested
by  the  Talmud,  and  the  Christian  paradise,  the  celestial
choirs  shadowed  forth  in  the  Apocalypse,  both  afford
scope  and  grounds  for  indulging  such  hopes.  But  the
Sufi  future  state  of  absorption  in  God  seems  entirely  to
exclude  them.  As  Tennyson  puts  it —

“ That  each,  who  seems  a separate  whole,

Should  move  his  rounds,  and  fusing  all
The  skirts  of  self  again,  should  fall
Remerging  in  the  general  Soul,

Is  faith  as  vague  as  all  unsweet.”

But  of  course  the  death  of  self  required  by  the  Sufi
system  necessarily  involved  the  sacrifice  of  all  human
affections.  Their  all-absorbing  love  of  God  left  no  room
for  love  of  aught  else.

According  to  the  Sufi  view,  some  foretaste  of  the  ulti-
mate “ union  with  God  ” may  be  obtained  in  the  pre-
sent life  in  those  states  of  spiritual  exaltation  known  as
“ ecstasy.”  This  “ ecstasy  ” (Hal),  and  the  raptures  (Zauq
and  Muwajid)  which  attended  it,  were  condemned  as  heresy
(Bid’ at)?  but  continued  to  be  held  by  the  Sulis  notwith-

froth  and  foam  of  mysticism.

2 Haji  Khalfa,  ii.  471 ; Ibn  Khalliqan,  i.  365.

xxxvu


standing.  The  poet  says  these  ecstatic  states  must  be
experienced  to  be  understood.  The  accounts  given  of
them  naturally  vary.  Some  describe  fand  as  a sort  of
“ thinking  away  self.”  Thus  Lahijl  says  it  is  to  “ emerge
from  the  limitations  of  self  which  veil  man’s  real  essence
(God).”  1 Imam  Ghazali  describes  fand  as  a “ prayer  of
rapture.”  “ In  that  state  man  is  effaced  from  self,  so
that  he  is  conscious  neither  of  his  body  nor  of  outward
things,  nor  of  inward  feelings.  He  is  1 rapt  ’ from  all
these,  journeying  first  to  his  Lord,  and  then  in  his  Lord ;
and  if  the  thought  that  he  is  effaced  from  self  occurs
to  him,  that  is  a defect.  The  highest  state  is  to  be
effaced  from  effacement.” ' Shabistari  describes  it  as
an  approximation  to  the  Light  of  lights,  which  blinds
the  mental  eye  with  excess  of  light.3  Ruml,  pursuing
his  favourite  metaphor  of  human  love,  describes  the
state  of  “fand  ” as  one  in  which  “ the  flame  of  ever-
lasting love  doth  burn  ere  it  transforms,” 4 — utterly
consuming  “ self  ” ere  it  quickens  the  lover  with  the
embrace  of  “ union.”

This  doctrine  of  ecstasy  is  the  really  mischievous  part
of  the  poet’s  system.  Like  Ghazali,5  he  was  aware  of  its
liability  to  abuse,6  and  for  ordinary  persons  he  prescribes
a certain  safeguard  in  the  person  of  the  “ Spiritual
Director”  (Pir).  When,  however,  he  has  to  deal  with
what  Cardinal  Newman  calls  the  “ eccentricities  of  the
saints,” — with  the  excesses  of  the  “ Pits  ” themselves, —
his  theology  blinds  his  moral  judgment,  and  he  does
not  dare  to  censure  even  such  outrageous  pretensions  as
those  he  relates  of  the  saint  Bayazld.  He  goes  so  far  as

xxxviii  INTRODUCTION.

to  say  that  in  the  mouth  of  a saint  even  infidelity  and
blasphemy  may  be  true  piety.  Such  are  the  pitfalls  that
lie  in  the  road  of  all  who  regard  religion  as  something
beyond  human  criticism,  and  ignore  Bishop  Butler’s  dictum,
that  reason  cannot  abdicate  its  right  of  judging  obvious
improprieties  in  religious  doctrines  and  persons.

As  regards  love  to  man,  the  poet  enjoins  the  practice
of  meekness,  patience,  humility,  kindness,  tolerance, —
in  fact,  of  all  the  virtues  classed  by  St.  Paul  as
“ charity  ” and  constituting  the  “ Christian  temper,”
though,  by  the  way,  it  is  hardly  confined  to  Christians.
But,  as  before  remarked,  his  love  to  man  does  not
amount  to  universal  philanthropy  like  that  of  St.
Francis.  With  him,  as  with  most  ascetics,  his  own
individual  salvation  is  the  paramount  object,  and  love
of  man  was  only  regarded  as  a means  to  attain  that
paramount  object.

Throughout  the  Masnavi  it  is  very  noticeable  how
the  poet  never  throws  aside  the  authority  of  the
Koran  and  the  Sunna,  but,  on  the  contrary,  presses
it  into  his  service  on  every  possible  occasion.  This
he  does  by  the  method  of  allegorical  interpretation
which  has  been  always  followed  by  Christian  mystics.
This  method  was  first  employed  by  the  Jew  Philo,
who  by  its  aid  read  much  of  Greek  philosophy  into
the  Old  Testament.  The  Christian  Platonist  Origen
copied  his  example,  and  ascribed  three  senses  to  Scrip-
ture. Mediaeval  mystical  writers  added  a fourth  sense,
and  laid  down  the  rule —

“ Littera  gesta  refert,  quod  credas  AllegorTa,

Moralis  quod  agas,  quod  speres  Anagogia.-’’

Itumi  goes  still  further,  and  ascribes  no  less  than
seven  senses  to  each  passage  of  the  Koran.

Also  noteworthy  is  his  view  of  the  evolution  of  man
out  of  inanimate  matter,  through  the  grades  of  the
vegetative  and  animal  souls,  up  to  humanity,  and  the


INTRODUCTION.


XXXIX


presage  and  augury  thence  derived  that  this  ascending
process  will  not  cease  at  death,  but  will  be  carried  on,
and  the  “ great  aspiration  of  humanity  ” realised  in  a
yet  higher  life  to  come.  His  doctriue  of  the  final  res-
titution of  all  who  at  the  last  judgment  throw  them-
selves unreservedly  on  the  mercy  of  the  Great  Judge,
is  also  remarkable.  This,  as  well  as  his  doctrine  of
“Reserve,”  he  probably  took  from  the  Christian  Platonists.1

It  seems  from  the  story  of  the  “Twelve  Volumes  of
Theology,”  that  Rumi  must  have  had  some  general  know-
ledge of  the  Epistles.  Possibly  he  might  have  been
attracted  by  St.  Paul  had  he  studied  him  with  an  open
mind,  unbiassed  by  the  unfavourable  accounts  given  of
him  in  Muhammadan  writers.2

It  need  hardly  be  said  that  Rumi  shows  all  an  en-
thusiast's fear  and  contempt  of  “ carnal  reason.”  The
emotions  chafe  against  the  control  of  reason  quite  as
much  as  the  passions  do,  and  according  to  Plato,  “ there
is  an  ancient  quarrel  between  poetry  and  philosophy.

IV.

LIFE  OF  THE  POET.

The  poet  was  born  at  Balkh  in  604  A.H.  (1207  a.d.),
of  a noble  family  claiming  descent  from  the  Khalifa  Abu
Bakr,  and  related  to  the  reigning  Khwarazmiau  dynasty.
His  father,  Bahau-’d-Dln,  had  to  quit  Balkh  about  1 2 1 1
a.d.  in  consequence  of  having  given  offence  to  the  king
by  a sermon  on  politics.  At  Xishapur  the  fugitives  met
the  great  Sufi  saint  Farldu-’d-Dln  ’Attar,  who  presented
the  child  Jalalu-’d-Dln  with  a copy  of  his  “ Asrdmama,”
and  said  to  his  father,  “ The  day  will  come  when  this
child  will  kindle  the  fire  of  divine  enthusiasm  throughout
the  world.”3  When  they  reached  Bagdad  they  heard

2 Shahrastani  (Cureton’s  ed.,  p.  s Ouseley,  “Persian  Poets,”  p.
173),  evidently  following  some  Juda-  1 13.

d


xl


INTRODUCTION.


the  news  of  the  destruction  of  Balkh  by  Chingiz  Khan.
After  further  wanderings  the  family  finally  settled  at
Qonla,  the  Iconium  of  St.  Paul,  in  the  old  Homan  pro-
vince of  “ Galatia,” 1 and  hence  the  poet  acquired  his
ethnic  name  of  RumI  or  the  “ Roman.”  Qonla  was  then
the  capital  of  ’Alau-’d-Dln  Kaiqubad,  one  of  the  western
branch  of  the  Saljuks,  who  bore  the  title  of  Sultan  of
Rum.  Here  the  poet  passed  his  life,  engaged  in  teaching
his  disciples,  the  order  of  Darveshes  called  “ Maulavis,”  2
and  in  writing  the  Masnavi  and  many  odes  and  hymns
for  their  spiritual  benefit.3  He  prescribed  the  use  of
music  in  their  religious  services,  because  he  found  that
the  sluggish  temperament  of  the  “ Romans  ” needed  this
stimulus.4  He  also  authorised  the  use  of  religious  dances,
which  had  been  practised  by  other  Sufi  sects  before  him.°
The  anecdotes  translated  by  Mr.  Redhouse  show  that  he
gave  way  at  times  to  excesses  of  wild  enthusiasm,  but
the  “ eccentricities  of  the  saints  ” are  not  very  edifying
reading.  They  only  serve  to  show  what  tricks  over-
wrought religious  feeling  will  play  with  well-meaning
men  when  uncontrolled  by  reason.  The  poet  was
assisted  in  composing  the  Masnavi  by  Hasan  Hasamu-’d-
Dln,  and  he  was  also  intimate  with  Burhanu-’d-Dln  of
Tirmld,  and  with  Shamsu-’d-Dln  of  Tabriz,  whose  name  lie
uses  as  his  poetic  title  ( taJchallus ).  He  died  at  Qonla  in
672  a.h.  (1273  A.D.),  leaving  a son,  Bahau-’d-Din  Valad,
who  succeeded  him  in  the  generalship  of  the  Maulavi
order.  His  dying  instructions  were  in  these  words : —

“ My  testament  is  this,  that  ye  be  pious  towards  God,
in  private  and  in  public ; — that  ye  eat  little,  sleep  little,
speak  little ; — that  ye  depart  from  wickedness  and  sin  ;

V.

THE  TRANSLATION.

The  Masnavi  contains  some  26,000  couplets,  and  a
full  translation  of  the  whole  poem  would  be  found  in-
tolerably tedious.  Up  to  the  present  time  the  most
sustained  attempts  in  that  direction  have  been  those  of
Professor  George  Rosen  (“  Mesnewi,”  Leipzig,  1849)  and
of  Mr.  Redhouse  (“The  Mesnevi,”  London,  1881).  The
poem,  consisting  as  it  does  of  stories  serving  as  pegs
on  which  to  hang  spiritual  morals,  easily  lends  itself  to
abridgment  in  its  purely  narrative  portions,  and  the
plan  adopted  in  the  present  translation  has  been  to  give
abridgments  of  the  principal  stories,  and  full  translations
of  the  main  doctrinal  morals  deduced  from  them.  Thus
Book  I.,  which,  in  Mr.  Redhouse’s  translation,  occupies
290  pages,  has  been  compressed  so  as  to  occupy  only  60
pages,  and  the  remaining  five  books  have  been  compressed
in  like  proportions.  Of  course  this  plan  is  open  to  the
objection  that  it  involves  the  omission  of  many  passages
worth  full  translation ; but  that  cannot  be  helped.

The  poem  is  written  in  a simple  and  unaffected  style,2
and  is  not  disfigured  by  the  laboured  prettiness  and  fine
writing  ( kalambazl ) which  are  found  in  JamI  and  later
Persian  poets.  Nevertheless  it  is  not  always  easy  to
follow  the  meaning.  When  the  poet  is  telling  his  stories

1 Nafahatu’-l  Uns  (Lees’  ed.),  p.  possesses  for  the  Pahlavi  dialect  the

534.  authority  of  the  Koran.  Bloch -

2 According  to  JamI,  the  Masnavi  maun’s  Prosody,  p.  xv.


aslii


INTRODUCTION.


lie  is  constantly  “going  off  upon  a word,” — drawing  a
fresh  moral  from  every  incident  in  the  course  of  the
story.  “ II  moralise  tout”  some  French  critic  says  of
him,  and  this  unfortunate  habit  of  his  is  certainly  some-
what of  a trial  to  his  readers.

The  pages  given  at  the  head  of  each  section  are  those
of  the  Lucknow  edition  of  1282  A.H.,  as  that  is  one  of
the  editions  commonly  used  in  India ; but  the  transla-
tion has  been  made  from  the  Bulaq  edition  of  1268  a.h.,
which  gives  correcter  readings,  and  omits  the  interpola-
tions often  occurring  in  the  Lucknow  edition,  and  likewise
in  the  Tabriz  edition  of  1264  a.h.

I have  made  use  of  the  Persian  commentary  of  ’Abdu-
l-’Ali  Muhammad,  published  at  Lucknow  in  1293  a.h.
I have  also  occasionally  referred  (so  far  as  my  very
limited  knowledge  of  Turkish  allowed  me)  to  the  great
Turkish  commentary  written  by  Ismail  Anquravl,  which
was  printed  at  Stambfil  in  1872.  Ismail  was  the  old
Maulavi  Darvesh,  familiarly  known  as  “ Fir  Dadd,”  who,
according  to  Haji  Khalfa,  invented  the  seventh  book  of
the  poem  (p.  329  infra).  As  he  was  a Fir,  or  spiritual
director  of  the  Maulavi  order,  his  interpretations  may  be
presumed  to  be  those  generally  accepted  by  the  brethren.

I have  also  to  acknowledge  my  obligations  to  Mr.
Eedhouse’s  “ Mesnevi,”  (which  contains  Bk.  I.),  and  to
Dr.  Tholuck’s  “ Ssufismus,”  and  “ Bllithensammlung  aus
den  Morgenliindischen  Mystik.”  Tholuck  was  an  indif-
ferent Persian  scholar,  and  many  of  his  translations  are
wrong,  but  he  grasped  the  meaning  of  Sufism  and  its
affinity  to  European  mysticism  much  more  thoroughly
than  many  who  were  far  superior  to  him  in  mere  verbal
scholarship.  The  value  of  his  work  was  fully  recog-
nised by  Dr.  Pusey,  a very  competent  judge,  in  his
“ Preface  to  Nicoll’s  Catalogue  of  Arabic  MSS.”  in  the
Bodleian  Library.


THE  MASNAVI.


Book  i.

PROLOGUE.

Hearken  to  the  reed-flute,  how  it  discourses
When  complaining  of  the  pains  of  separation —

“ Ever  since  they  tore  me  from  my  osier  bed,

My  plaintive  notes  have  moved  men  and  women  to  tears.

I burst  my  breast,  striving  to  give  vent  to  sighs,

And  to  express  the  pangs  of  my  yearning  for  my  home.
He  who  abides  far  away  from  his  home
Is  ever  longing  for  the  day  he  shall  return.

My  wailing  is  heard  in  every  throng,

In  concert  with  them  that  rejoice  and  them  that  weep.
Each  interprets  my  notes  in  harmony  with  his  own  feelings,
But  not  one  fathoms  the  secrets  of  my  heart.

My  secrets  are  not  alien  from  my  plaintive  notes,

Yet  they  are  not  manifest  to  the  sensual  ear.

Body  is  not  veiled  from  soul,  neither  soul  from  body,

Yet  no  man  hath  ever  seen  a soul.”

This  plaint  of  the  flute  is  fire,  not  mere  air.

Let  him  who  lacks  this  fire  be  accounted  dead !

Tis  the  fire  of  love  that  inspires  the  flute,1
’Tis  the  ferment  of  love  that  possesses  the  wine.

The  flute  is  the  confidant  of  all  unhappy  lovers ;

1 Love  signifies  the  strong  attraction  that  draws  all  creatures  back  to
reunion  with  their  Creator.

A


2


THE  M A SNA  VI.


[book  i.


Yea,  its  strains  lay  bare  my  inmost  secrets.

Who  hath  seen  a poison  and  an  antidote  like  the  flute  ?
Who  hath  seen  a sympathetic  consoler  like  the  flute  ?

The  flute  tells  the  tale  of  love’s  bloodstained  path,

It  recounts  the  story  of  Maj  nun’s  love  toils.

None  is  privy  to  these  feelings  save  one  distracted,

As  ear  inclines  to  the  whispers  of  the  tongue.

Through  grief  my  days  are  as  labour  and  sorrow,

My  days  move  on,  hand  in  hand  with  anguish.

Yet,  though  my  days  vanish  thus,  ’tis  no  matter,

Do  thou  abide,  0 Incomparable  Pure  One  ! 1

But  all  who  are  not  fishes  are  soon  tired  of  water ;

And  they  who  lack  daily  bread  find  the  day  very  long  ;

So  the  “ Raw”  comprehend  not  the  state  of  the  “ Ripe  ; ” 2
Therefore  it  behoves  me  to  shorten  my  discourse.

Arise,  0 son  ! burst  thy  bonds  and  be  free  !

How  long  wilt  thou  be  captive  to  silver  and  gold  ?

Though  thou  pour  the  ocean  into  thy  pitcher,

It  can  hold  no  more  than  one  day’s  store.

The  pitcher  of  the  desire  of  the  covetous  never  fills,

The  oyster-shell  fills  not  with  pearls  till  it  is  content ;
Only  he  whose  garment  is  rent  by  the  violence  of  love
Is  wholly  pure  from  covetousness  and  sin.

Hail  to  thee,  then,  0 LOVE,  sweet  madness!

Thou  who  healest  all  our  infirmities !

Who  art  the  physician  of  our  pride  and  self-conceit !

Who  art  our  Plato  and  our  Galen  !

Love  exalts  our  earthly  bodies  to  heaven,

And  makes  the  very  bills  to  dance  with  joy !

0 lover,  ’twas  love  that  gave  life  to  Mount  Sinai,

When  “it  quaked,  and  Moses  fell  down  in  a swoon.”3
Did  my  Beloved  only  touch  me  with  his  lips,

1 too,  like  the  flute,  would  burst  out  in  melody.

But  he  who  is  parted  from  them  that  speak  his  tongue,
Though  he  possess  a hundred  voices,  is  perforce  dumb.
When  the  rose  has  faded  and  the  garden  is  withered,

The  song  of  the  nightingale  is  no  longer  to  be  heard.

The  Beloved  is  all  in  all,  the  lover  only  veils  Him  ; 1
The  Beloved  is  all  that  lives,  the  lover  a dead  thing.
When  the  lover  feels  no  longer  Love’s  quickening,

He  becomes  like  a bird  who  has  lost  its  wings.  Alas !
How  can  I retain  my  senses  about  me,

When  the  Beloved  shows  not  the  light  of  His  countenance?

Love  desires  that  this  secret  should  be  revealed,

For  if  a mirror  reflects  not,  of  what  use  is  it  ?

Knowest  thou  why  thy  mirror  reflects  not  ?

Because  the  rust  has  not  been  scoured  from  its  face.

If  it  were  purified  from  all  rust  and  defilement,

It  would  reflect  the  shining  of  the  SUN  of  God.2

0 friends,  ye  have  now  heard  this  tale,

Which  sets  forth  the  very  essence  of  my  case.

Story  I.  The  Prince  and  the  Handmaid  (p.  5).

A prince,  while  engaged  on  a hunting  excursion,
espied  a fair  maiden,  and  by  promises  of  gold  induced
her  to  accompany  him.  After  a time  she  fell  sick,  and
the  prince  had  her  tended  by  divers  physicians.  As,
however,  they  all  omitted  to  say,  “ God  willing,1  we  will
cure  her,”  their  treatment  was  of  no  avail.  So  the  prince
offered  prayer,  and  in  answer  thereto  a physician  was
sent  from  heaven.  He  at  once  condemned  his  pre-
decessors’ view  of  the  case,  and  by  a very  skilful
diagnosis,  discovered  that  the  real  cause  of  the  maiden’s
illness  was  her  love  for  a certain  goldsmith  of  Samarcand.
In  accordance  with  the  physician’s  advice,  the  prince  sent
to  Samarcand  and  fetched  the  goldsmith,  and  married
him  to  the  lovesick  maiden,  and  for  six  months  the  pair
lived  together  in  the  utmost  harmony  and  happiness.
At  the  end  of  that  period  the  physician,  by  divine
command,  gave  the  goldsmith  a poisonous  draught,  which
caused  his  strength  and  beauty  to  decay,  and  he  then  lost
favour  with  the  maiden,  and  she  was  reunited  to  the
king.  This  divine  command  was  precisely  similar  to
God’s  command  to  Abraham  to  slay  his  son  Ishmael,  .and
to  the  act  of  the  angel  in  slaying  the  servant  of  Moses.2
and  is  therefore  beyond  human  criticism.

One  cannot  converse  with  a strict  iv.  15.

STORY  i.]  THE  PRINCE  AND  THE  HANDMAID.


Description  of  Love  (p.  7).

A true  lover  is  proved  such  by  his  pain  of  heart ;

No  sickness  is  there  like  sickness  of  heart.

The  lover’s  ailment  is  different  from  all  ailments ;

Love  is  the  astrolabe  of  God’s  mysteries.

A lover  may  hanker  after  this  love  or  that  love,

But  at  the  last  he  is  drawn  to  the  King  of  love.
However  much  we  describe  and  explain  love,

When  we  fall  in  love  we  are  ashamed  of  our  words.
Explanation  by  the  tongue  makes  most  things  clear,
But  love  unexplained  is  clearer.

When  pen  hasted  to  write,

On  reaching  the  subject  of  love  it  split  in  twain.

When  the  discourse  touched  on  the  matter  of  love,

Pen  was  broken  and  paper  torn.

In  explaining  it  Reason  sticks  fast,  as  an  ass  in  mire  ;
Naught  but  Love  itself  can  explain  love  and  lovers !
None  but  the  sun  can  display  the  sun,

If  you  would  see  it  displayed,  turn  not  away  from  it.
Shadows,  indeed,  may  indicate  the  sun’s  presence,

But  only  the  sun  displays  the  light  of  life.

Shadows  induce  slumber,  like  evening  talks,

But  when  the  sun  arises  the  “ moon  is  split  asunder.'' 1
In  the  world  there  is  naught  so  wondrous  as  the  sun,
But  the  Sun  of  the  soul  sets  not  and  has  no  yesterday.
Though  the  material  sun  is  unique  and  single,

We  can  conceive  similar  suns  like  to  it.

But  the  Sun  of  the  soul,  beyond  this  firmament. —

No  like  thereof  is  seen  in  concrete  or  abstract.2
Where  is  there  room  in  conception  for  His  essence,

So  that  similitudes  of  Him  should  be  conceivable  ?

1 Koran  liv.  I.

2 There  is  a tradition,  “ I know  my  Lord  by  my  Lord.”

THE  MASNA  VI.


[book  i.


Shamsu-d-Din  importunes  Jalalu-’d-Din
to  compose  the  Masnavi  (p.  7).

The  sun  (Shams)  of  Tabriz  is  a perfect  light,

A sun,  yea,  one  of  the  beams  of  God !

When  the  praise  was  heard  of  the  “ Sun  of  Tabriz,”

The  sun  of  the  fourth  heaven  bowed  its  head.

Now  that  I have  mentioned  his  name,  it  is  but  right
To  set  forth  some  indications  of  his  beneficence.

That  precious  Soul  caught  my  skirt,

Smelling  the  perfume  of  the  garment  of  Yusuf;

And  said,  “ For  the  sake  of  our  ancient  friendship,

Tell  forth  a hint  of  those  sweet  states  of  ecstasy,

That  earth  and  heaven  may  be  rejoiced,

And  also  Reason  and  Spirit,  a hundredfold.”

I said,  “ 0 thou  who  art  far  from  ‘ The  Friend,’

Like  a sick  man  who  has  strayed  from  his  physician,
Importune  me  not,  for  I am  beside  myself ;

My  understanding  is  gone,  I cannot  sing  praises.
Whatsoever  one  says,  whose  reason  is  thus  astray,

Let  him  not  boast ; his  efforts  are  useless.

Whatever  he  says  is  not  to  the  point,

And  is  clearly  inapt  and  wide  of  the  mark.

What  can  I say  when  not  a nerve  of  mine  is  sensible  ?
Can  I explain  ‘The  Friend’  to  one  to  whom  He  is  no
Friend  ?

Verily  my  singing  His  praise  were  dispraise,

For  ’twould  prove  me  existent,  and  existence  is  error.1
Can  I describe  my  separation  and  my  bleeding  heart  ?
Nay,  put  off  this  matter  till  another  season.”

He  said,  “Feed  me,  for  I am  an  hungred,

And  at  once,  for  ‘ the  time  is  a sharp  sword.’

0 comrade,  the  Sufi  is  ‘the  son  of  time  present.’ -

It  is  not  the  rule  of  his  canon  to  say,  ‘To-morrow.’

Can  it  be  that  thou  art  not  a true  Sufi  ?

Heady  money  is  lost  by  giving  credit.”

I said,  “ ’Tis  best  to  veil  the  secrets  of  ‘ The  Friend.’

So  give  good  heed  to  the  morals  of  these  stories.

That  is  better  than  that  the  secrets  of  ‘ The  Friend  ’
Should  be  noised  abroad  in  the  talk  of  strangers.”

He  said,  “Without  veil  or  covering  or  deception.

Speak  out,  and  vex  me  not,  0 man  of  many  words !

Strip  off  the  veil  and  speak  out,  for  do  not  I
Enter  under  the  same  coverlet  as  the  Beloved  ? ”

I said,  “If  the  Beloved  were  exposed  to  outward
view,

Neither  wouldst  thou  endure,  nor  embrace,  nor  form.
Press  thy  suit,  yet  with  moderation ;

A blade  of  grass  cannot  pierce  a mountain.

If  the  sun  that  illumines  the  world

Were  to  draw  nigher,  the  world  would  be  consumed.1

Close  thy  mouth  and  shut  the  eyes  of  this  matter,

That  the  world’s  life  be  not  made  a bleeding  heart.

No  longer  seek  this  peril,  this  bloodshed  ;

Hereafter  impose  silence  on  the  ‘ Sun  of  Tabriz.’  ”

He  said,  “ Thy  words  are  endless.  Now  tell  forth
All  thy  story  from  its  beginning.”


Story  II.  The  Oilman  and  his  Parrot  (p.  io).

him  with  its  agreeable  prattle,  and  to  watch  his  shop
when  he  went  out.  One  day,  when  the  parrot  was  alone
in  the  shop,  a cat  upset  one  of  the  oil-jars.  When  the
